## MILP / optimisation formulation for battery dispatch (GB behind-the-meter C&I lithium-ion, 0.1-5 MW, 1-4h)

| Constant | Value | Units | Basis / conditions | Source | Caveat |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| AC-AC round-trip efficiency, grid-scale Li-ion (modelling assumption) | 92% | % (eta_b * eta_s) | Uniform assumption applied across 1h-5h duration scenarios for a 1 MWh system; authors align it to a 2-hour Tesla Megapack specification. Vintage 2023 data / 2025 paper. | Hornek, T., Lee, Y., Potenciano Menci, S., Pavic, I., 'The Value of Battery Energy Storage in the Continuous Intraday Market: Forecast vs. Perfect Foresight Strategies', IEEE PowerTech 2025, Section IV-B, arXiv:2501.07121v2, DOI 10.1109/POWERTECH59965.2025.11180315 | A single flat number; the same paper does not model part-load efficiency, which the measurement-based literature shows is materially lower at low dispatch. |
| Charge and discharge efficiency, case-study BES | 95% each (round-trip 90.25%) | % | 20 MW / 12.5 MWh Li(NiMnCo)O2 18650 system, cell temperature 25 C, ISO-NE market simulation, 2015 price data. | Xu, B., Zhao, J., Zheng, T., Litvinov, E., Kirschen, D.S., IEEE Transactions on Power Systems 33(2):2248-2259, 2018, Section V-A | DC-side; does not state whether auxiliaries are included. |
| Measured overall AC-AC round-trip efficiency versus power (part load) | ~91% peak at roughly one third of rated power; ~87% at rated power (36 kW); falls below 60% at very low power (<~2 kW); round-trip loss factor exceeds 50% below ~2 kW | % efficiency vs kW AC | Sony/muRata Fortelion LFP cell, 36 kWh pack at 665.6 VDC nominal, Siemens Sinamics S120 bidirectional inverter rated 36 kW, measured at 600-750 VDC and 0-100% of nominal power in both directions. Laboratory-parametrised, 2019. | Hesse, H.C., Kumtepeli, V., Schimpe, M., Reniers, J., Howey, D., Tripathi, A., Wang, Y., Jossen, A., Energies 12(6):999, 2019, Figs 3-4, DOI 10.3390/en12060999 | Small-scale (36 kW) hardware; the shape generalises but the exact numbers do not transfer to a 1-5 MW container without re-parametrisation. Excludes EMS and thermal management parasitics, which the authors explicitly exclude. |
| Battery ohmic resistances (LFP cell) | R_charge = 46.66 mOhm; R_discharge = 50.29 mOhm; U_OCV = 3.30 V | mOhm, V | muRata/Sony Fortelion LFP cell, equivalent circuit model with single resistance, 25 C, SOC dependence of OCV neglected within 10-95% SOC. | Hesse, H.C. et al., Energies 12(6):999, 2019, Section 2.5 | Cell-level; scaling to pack level requires the series/parallel configuration. |
| Cycle-depth stress function (NMC 18650) | Phi(delta) = 5.24e-4 * delta^2.03 | % of life lost per full cycle, with delta the cycle depth as a fraction in [0,1] | Li(NiMnCo)O2-based 18650 cells at 25 C; consistent with the stated 3000 cycles at 80% cycle depth (Phi(0.8) = 3.32e-4, i.e. ~3010 cycles to 100% life loss). | Xu, B. et al., IEEE Transactions on Power Systems 33(2):2248-2259, 2018, eq. (24) | NMC 18650 chemistry, pre-2018. Modern GB grid installations are predominantly LFP modules, for which no equivalent public 2025-26 stress function was found. |
| Non-linearity of cycle life with depth of discharge | Over 50,000 cycles at 10% cycle depth (35 kWh lifetime throughput) versus 500 cycles at 100% cycle depth (3.5 kWh lifetime throughput) | cycles; kWh lifetime throughput | A single 7 Wh NMC cell, laboratory data. | Xu, B. et al., IEEE Transactions on Power Systems 33(2):2248-2259, 2018, Section II-B-1 | A 10x difference in lifetime throughput for the same cell — this is the reason a flat per-MWh degradation cost is unsafe. |
| Effect of omitting cycle-aging cost from the dispatch objective | Annual prorated profit -$2101.3k (no aging cost) vs +$222.5k (1-segment) vs +$276.3k (16-segment); battery life expectancy 1.1 vs 8.2 vs 8.0 years | thousand USD per year; years | 20 MW / 12.5 MWh BES, ISO-NE SE-MASS zone, full-year 2015 real-time market with 5-minute settlement, battery pack replacement cost $300,000/MWh, perfect price foresight. | Xu, B. et al., IEEE Transactions on Power Systems 33(2):2248-2259, 2018, Table I | Day-ahead market figures are milder: -$775.0k / 2.9 years (no cost) vs +$10k / 9.7 years (16-segment). The magnitude of the error scales with price volatility, so a 2025-26 GB half-hourly market sits between these cases. |
| Piecewise-linearisation error of the cycle-aging cost versus ex-post rainflow | ~35% relative error with 1 cost segment, falling to negligible by 16 segments | % relative error | Full-year 5-minute ISO-NE RTM dispatch simulation; error measured as \|Chat - R*L\| / (R*L) against rainflow benchmark. | Xu, B. et al., IEEE Transactions on Power Systems 33(2):2248-2259, 2018, Fig. 5 and eq. (26) | Requires the marginal cost curve to be convex (c_1 <= c_2 <= ... <= c_J) for Theorem 1 to hold. |
| Battery pack replacement cost (used for degradation pricing) | $300,000/MWh (Xu et al., 2015-17 vintage); EUR 500/kWh base and EUR 250/kWh future scenario (Hesse et al., 2019 vintage) | USD/MWh; EUR/kWh | Xu: Li(NiMnCo)O2 18650 pack, ISO-NE case study. Hesse: LFP, retail cost 'approximately 20% below recent single-unit pricing information (excluding VAT)'. | Xu, B. et al., IEEE TPWRS 33(2):2248-2259, 2018, Section V-A; Hesse, H.C. et al., Energies 12(6):999, 2019, Section 2.3 | Both are badly out of date for 2026 GB pricing; they are cited for the FORM of the degradation cost, not the level. The degradation cost per MWh throughput must be re-derived from a current replacement cost. |
| End-of-life state of health threshold | 80% (k_EOL = 0.8) | % of nominal capacity | Stated as 'in accordance with the literature'; used in the degradation cost prorating C_usage = (-dSOH/(1-k_EOL)) * C_inv_bat. | Hesse, H.C. et al., Energies 12(6):999, 2019, Section 2.3 | A convention, not a physical constant; warranty documents may specify a different threshold. |
| Calendar (shelf) ageing rate | 10% of life per year (10-year shelf life) | % life loss per year | Li(NiMnCo)O2 cells at 25 C maintained cell temperature; combined with cycle loss as L_exp = 100% / (dL_cal + dL_cycle). | Xu, B. et al., IEEE Transactions on Power Systems 33(2):2248-2259, 2018, Section V-A and V-D | Strongly temperature-dependent; a GB outdoor container without active cooling will not match a 25 C laboratory rate. |
| Usable state-of-charge window (published case studies) | 15%-95% (Xu et al.); 10%-95% with C-rate <= 1 (Hesse et al.); 15%-85% (Garifi et al.) | % of nominal energy | Xu: 20 MW/12.5 MWh NMC grid system. Hesse: 36 kWh LFP stationary system. Garifi: 5 kWh residential system, restricted explicitly 'to preserve the ESS lifetime'. | Xu, B. et al., IEEE TPWRS 33(2):2248-2259, 2018, Section V-A; Hesse, H.C. et al., Energies 12(6):999, 2019, Section 2.2; Garifi, K. et al., arXiv:1805.00100v2, 2018, Section V | Sources disagree by up to 15 percentage points on usable depth; the value is a warranty/vendor parameter, not a physical constant, and must come from the specific product datasheet. |
| Cyclic capacity fade versus C-rate (LFP, measured) | ~4e-4 %/h at rated power (C-rate 1 charging, 36 kW); rising to ~8e-4 %/h above rated | % capacity fade per hour | muRata/Sony Fortelion LFP, in-house laboratory measurements, 25 C, charge direction; discharge fade is markedly lower and flatter. | Hesse, H.C. et al., Energies 12(6):999, 2019, Fig. 3b | Charge-direction fade dominates and rises sharply above C-rate 1, which is the quantitative justification for a C-rate cap. |
| Perfect-foresight premium, hourly wholesale arbitrage | A two-week backcast dispatch rule captured 'about 85% or more' of perfect-foresight arbitrage value in each of six years; year-by-year roughly 84.5% (2003) to 89.5% (2005) | % of perfect-foresight value | PJM load-weighted average hourly prices 2002-2007, 12-hour storage device, 80% round-trip efficiency, price-taker. The backcast uses NO forward-looking information — only the previous two weeks of prices — so the authors describe it as a lower bound on value capture. | Sioshansi, R., Denholm, P., Jenkin, T., Weiss, J., 'Estimating the value of electricity storage in PJM: Arbitrage and some welfare effects', Energy Economics 31(2):269-277, 2009, Section 3 and Fig. 6, DOI 10.1016/j.eneco.2008.10.005 | Hourly resolution, low-volatility 2000s US prices. GB 2025-26 half-hourly prices are far more volatile, which plausibly widens the gap; no GB-specific replication was found. |
| Perfect-foresight premium, continuous intraday market | Forecast-driven rolling-window strategy earned EUR 146,237 per year, 'only 11% below perfect foresight' | EUR/yr; % below perfect foresight | 1 MW / 1 MWh BESS, real 2023 German EPEX SPOT continuous intraday data, 8-hour rolling horizon re-optimised every 5 minutes, 92% round-trip efficiency, forecast = IDA price beyond 5h lead time and the average of the last four trades within 5h. | Hornek, T. et al., IEEE PowerTech 2025, abstract and Section V, arXiv:2501.07121v2 | German CID, not GB. The same approach beat the ID1 index by over 4% and ID3 by over 32%. |
| Perfect-foresight premium, UK | 75-95% of optimal profits obtained without perfect foresight of future prices | % of optimal profits | UK market, arbitrage and reserve; the same paper reports UK battery systems could triple profits by participating in the reserve market rather than arbitrage alone. | Staffell, I., Rustomji, M., 'Maximising the value of electricity storage', Journal of Energy Storage 8:212-225, 2016, DOI 10.1016/j.est.2016.08.010 | 2016 vintage, pre-dating Dynamic Containment, the Targeted Charging Review and the 2025 co-optimised auction. The reserve-versus-arbitrage conclusion in particular should not be carried forward. |
| Combined effect of ageing and conversion losses on arbitrage profit | Up to 36% reduction in attainable profit | % of profit | German intraday market data for 2017, LFP system with measured efficiency and ageing curves, most profitable arbitrage use case considered. | Hesse, H.C. et al., Energies 12(6):999, 2019, abstract and Section 4 | Excludes auxiliary/thermal management loads, so the true gross-to-net gap is larger. |
| Gross arbitrage revenue versus primary control reserve revenue (comparison case) | Up to 8,762 EUR per MWh installed capacity per month (intraday arbitrage, gross) versus 7,716 EUR per MWh per month (primary control reserve), same sample month | EUR/MWh installed capacity/month | German 2017 intraday market data; PCR reference case simulated with SimSES using weekly pay-as-bid tender results. | Hesse, H.C. et al., Energies 12(6):999, 2019, abstract | Gross of losses and degradation. German market, 2017 — not transferable to GB levels, cited only for the relative ordering and the fact that ancillary and arbitrage were of similar magnitude before costs. |
| German primary control reserve weekly tender prices (reference) | 3,724 EUR/MW (2015 yearly average); 2,494 EUR/MW (2016); 2,489 EUR/MW (2017); 2017 weekly minimum 2,094, maximum 2,510 | EUR/MW per week | Mean winning-bid prices from weekly pay-as-bid PCR auctions, publicly available market data. | Hesse, H.C. et al., Energies 12(6):999, 2019, Table 1 | German, 2015-2017; included only to show the historical decline in frequency-response prices as capacity saturated — the same saturation dynamic has since played out in GB Dynamic Containment. |
| Reserve capacity gain from an optimised recharging policy versus a heuristic | 6.37 kW versus 4.66 kW for the same battery (a 37% increase), at a constraint-violation probability of 1e-4 | kW of primary frequency reserve | 10 kWh / 7 kW residential battery, initial charge 5 kWh, round-trip efficiency 90% (eta_c = eta_d = sqrt(0.90)), Continental Europe frequency data 2014-2016 at 1 s resolution (1091 complete days), 15-minute time step, 96-step horizon, Delta_f_max = 200 mHz. | Engels, J., Claessens, B., Deconinck, G., IEEE Transactions on Smart Grid 10(2):1971-1981, 2019, Section VI-A-1, arXiv:1710.08331 | Continental Europe FCR, 15-minute steps. The 0.63 kW of the 7 kW used for recharging is the direct analogue of GB state-of-charge management energy. |
| Reserve capacity per unit energy as a function of C-rate | Concave in C-rate, saturating at roughly 4.2-4.5 kW per kWh at C-rates of about 5-6 | kW reserve per kWh of energy capacity | Round-trip efficiency 90%, violation probabilities 1e-3 to 1e-5, Continental Europe frequency data. | Engels, J. et al., IEEE Transactions on Smart Grid 10(2):1971-1981, 2019, Fig. 3, arXiv:1710.08331 | Shows that beyond a saturation C-rate, adding inverter power buys no further reserve capacity — the binding limit becomes energy. Directly relevant to 1h vs 4h duration selection for GB frequency products. |
| Scenario count needed for sample-average approximation convergence | Optimality gap below 3% from about 250 scenarios; below 1% from about 1000 scenarios; below 1% from about 500 backward-reduced scenarios | number of scenarios; % optimality gap | n_U = 1e5 upper-bound samples, n_L = 10 lower-bound solves, alpha = 0.005; CREST-generated residential demand and modelled PV profiles; Kantorovich-distance backward scenario reduction. | Engels, J. et al., IEEE Transactions on Smart Grid 10(2):1971-1981, 2019, Section VI-B-1, arXiv:1710.08331 | Residential self-consumption plus frequency control. A C&I BTM problem with more revenue streams will likely need more scenarios. |
| Chance-constraint violation probability used for frequency service delivery | epsilon on the order of 1e-4 to 1e-5 | probability of constraint violation | Chosen because breaching the constraint means the frequency control service cannot be delivered; the authors state 'the goal is to get epsilon on the order of 1e-4 or 1e-5'. | Engels, J. et al., IEEE Transactions on Smart Grid 10(2):1971-1981, 2019, Section IV-C, arXiv:1710.08331 | Monte Carlo verification requires O(n_delta/epsilon) samples, roughly 1e6 for epsilon = 1e-4, which is why the robust SOCP reformulation is used instead. |
| Gurobi default numerical tolerances | Primal and dual feasibility tolerance 1e-6; integrality tolerance 1e-5; relative MIP gap 1e-4 | dimensionless | Gurobi Optimizer documentation, current release. | Gurobi Optimization, 'Numerics Guide — Tolerances and User-Scaling' and 'Parameters' reference, current release, accessed 2026, https://docs.gurobi.com/projects/optimizer/en/current/concepts/numericguide/tolerances_scaling.html | With M = 1e6 and integrality tolerance 1e-5, a big-M constraint permits up to 10 units of leakage through a binary that the solver considers to be zero. |
| Gurobi recommended model coefficient ranges | Matrix coefficients within six orders of magnitude, ideally within [1e-3, 1e6]; right-hand sides and variable bounds on the order of 1e6 or less; objective values less than 1e6 and ideally above 1 | dimensionless ratios | Gurobi Optimizer documentation. | Gurobi Optimization, 'Numerics Guide — Tolerances and User-Scaling', current release, accessed 2026, https://docs.gurobi.com/projects/optimizer/en/current/concepts/numericguide/tolerances_scaling.html | Working in kW/kWh with GBP/MWh prices and a 17,520-period horizon can breach this; MW/MWh units are safer. |
| NESO frequency response product technical requirements (2025/2026) | Dynamic Containment: 0.5 s maximum response initiation, full delivery within 1 s, 15 minutes sustained. Dynamic Moderation: 1 s, up to 30 minutes. Dynamic Regulation: 10 s, up to 60 minutes. Static Firm Frequency Response: 30 s, 30 minutes, low frequency only. | seconds (response); minutes (sustain) | NESO official product documentation, GB, 2025/2026. | NESO, 'Frequency Response Services' and 'Dynamic Containment' product pages, accessed 2026, https://www.neso.energy/industry-information/balancing-services/frequency-response-services | NESO submitted an EBR Article 18 consultation to Ofgem on 1 May 2026 proposing changes to the Dynamic Response Services, decision expected July 2026 — these parameters may change within the underwriting horizon. |
| NESO Quick Reserve technical requirements | Full delivery 1 minute from instruction start time; activation period not exceeding 5 minutes; recovery period maximum 3 minutes | minutes | GB, Phase 1 live 19 November 2024 on the Enduring Auction Capability platform; Phase 2 first auction for non-BM units 2 September 2025. Positive and Negative variants (PQR/NQR). | NESO, 'Quick Reserve', accessed 2026, https://www.neso.energy/industry-information/balancing-services/reserve-services/quick-reserve | Non-BM units can participate, unlike Balancing Reserve — this matters for sub-1 MW BTM assets. |
| GB co-optimised day-ahead ancillary auction (2025/2026 structure) | Single simultaneous pay-as-clear day-ahead auction at 14:00 UK local time covering Dynamic Containment, Dynamic Moderation, Dynamic Regulation, Quick Reserve and Balancing Reserve; Slow Reserve added March 2026. Service windows of 30 minutes; Linked Service Windows with a default 2-hour minimum duration from January 2026. | clock time; minutes | Balancing Reserve moved from an 08:15 morning auction to the 14:00 co-optimised afternoon auction; final morning auction 28 October 2025, first co-optimised afternoon auction 29 October 2025. Clearing via the N-SIDE power matching algorithm. | NESO, 'Enduring Auction Capability (EAC)', accessed 2026, https://www.neso.energy/industry-information/balancing-services/enduring-auction-capability-eac ; NESO, 'Balancing Reserve', https://www.neso.energy/industry-information/balancing-services/reserve-services/balancing-reserve ; Ofgem, 'Decision to approve amendments to the Terms and Conditions relating to balancing for new Slow Reserve', October 2025 | This is the single largest structural break from pre-2024 GB literature. Any model assuming 4-hour EFA-block DC auctions or independent per-product clearing is describing a market that no longer exists. |
| Balancing Reserve eligibility thresholds | BM Unit registration in the Single Markets Platform required; minimum contract capacity 1 MW or above; control and/or system telephony required during all contracted windows | MW | NESO official service documentation, GB, 2025/2026. | NESO, 'Balancing Reserve', accessed 2026, https://www.neso.energy/industry-information/balancing-services/reserve-services/balancing-reserve | A 0.1-0.9 MW BTM asset cannot access Balancing Reserve on a standalone basis; aggregation is required. |
| GB market access enablers for behind-the-meter flexibility | Open Balancing Platform live 12 December 2023 (bulk dispatch of small BM units and batteries); BSC Modification P415 approved by Ofgem October 2023 and implemented 7 November 2024 (independent aggregators as Virtual Trading Parties accessing wholesale markets) | dates | GB regulatory and system-operator record. | NESO, 'First stages of Open Balancing Platform go live', 12 December 2023, https://www.neso.energy/news/first-stages-open-balancing-platform-go-live ; Elexon, 'Elexon implements landmark rule change to drive growth in flexibility', 7 November 2024, https://www.elexon.co.uk/2024/11/07/elexon-implements-landmark-rule-change-to-drive-growth-in-flexibility-and-progress-to-net-zero/ ; Ofgem, 'Decision on P415', October 2023 | Both post-date most academic GB BESS literature; models parameterised on pre-2024 Balancing Mechanism skip rates or pre-P415 route-to-market assumptions are stale. |
| Marginal arbitrage value of duration (concavity) | 9 to 10 hours of storage captures 90% of the maximum attainable arbitrage value, across round-trip efficiencies from 50% to 90% | hours of storage; % of maximum value | PJM 2006 average hourly prices, price-taking device. | Sioshansi, R. et al., Energy Economics 31(2):269-277, 2009, Section 2 and Fig. 5 | Hourly US prices from 2006. The implication for a 1-4 hour GB asset is that it sits on the steep part of the duration-value curve, so capacity fade costs revenue faster than proportionally — but the specific hours figure should not be transferred to GB. |
| Effect of round-trip efficiency on arbitrage value (super-linearity) | Raising a 20-hour device from 70% to 80% round-trip efficiency increases arbitrage value by more than 30%, from $60/kW-year to $80/kW-year | USD/kW-year; % | PJM 2006 average hourly prices. | Sioshansi, R. et al., Energy Economics 31(2):269-277, 2009, Section 2 | The mechanism (a less efficient device must charge in more, and progressively worse-priced, hours) is general; the magnitude depends on the price duration curve and will differ in GB. |
| Storage representation error from representative-day aggregation | An enhanced representative-periods method (transition matrix and cluster indices) achieves storage representation errors 'almost 10 times lower' than the traditional representative-day approach | relative error factor | Storage investment analysis with representative periods versus full chronology. | Tejada-Arango, D.A., Domeshek, M., Wogrin, S., Centeno, E., 'Enhanced Representative Days and System States Modeling for Energy Storage Investment Analysis', IEEE Transactions on Power Systems 33(6), 2018, DOI 10.1109/TPWRS.2018.2819578 | Stated as a ratio, not an absolute percentage; the absolute error of the traditional method depends on the case. It establishes the order of magnitude at stake, not a transferable number. |
| Elexon settlement and GB half-hourly period count | 48 settlement periods per day; 17,520 per non-leap year (46 and 50 on clock-change days); NESO ancillary service windows also 30 minutes | periods | GB Balancing and Settlement Code half-hourly settlement, aligned with NESO service window granularity. | NESO, 'Enduring Auction Capability (EAC)' (30-minute service windows), accessed 2026, https://www.neso.energy/industry-information/balancing-services/enduring-auction-capability-eac | Clock-change days have 46 and 50 periods; a model hard-coded to 48 will misalign price and load series by an hour for part of the year. |

| GB BESS project finance deal terms (observed) | Econergy Immingham 80MW/240MWh: c.£42.7m total, £38.4m main facility plus a VAT revolving facility and a debt-service reserve facility; ten-year EDF revenue floor covering c.50% of expected total revenues, of which Capacity Market is c.11%. Harmony Energy: £60m facility, five-year tenor, with a £70m accordion (later £130m total). Zenobe: £235m non-recourse from a five-bank club. Gresham House Holdco 1: £240m facility, £210m drawn, seven-year term with a fourteen-year amortisation profile, semi-annual covenant compliance certificates including debt service cover, borrowing capped at 50% of NAV at drawdown | £ and years | GB, 2023-2026 transactions. | Solar Power Portal, 'Santander provides debt finance for Econergy, Brockwell BESS', 2026; NatWest, 'The BESS market: delivering the backbone for a greener future'; Zenobe, '£235 million of non-recourse project finance debt from five banks'; Gresham House Energy Storage Fund plc, Annual Report 2025 | Margins and DSCR levels were not disclosed in any of these. Gearing for Immingham is not disclosed; a rough derivation against Modo's capex assumptions suggests roughly two-thirds, but this is inference, not fact. |
| Indicative DSCR and leverage by revenue structure | Tolling 1.20x P90; capacity-contracted 1.25x P90; hybrid hedge 1.30x P50 and 1.10x P90; pure merchant 1.40x P50. Senior debt 45-55% of capital for merchant, 60-65% for contracted. Alternative convention: contracted solar 1.20-1.30x P50, contracted wind 1.30-1.40x P50, merchant 1.75-2.00x P50, with a 1.00x P99 test | x coverage; % of capital structure | United States, 2026 (SOFR-referenced, ITC-inclusive structures). The second set is generic renewable project finance, not storage-specific. | SunRaise Capital (N. Jovanelly), 'Standalone Battery Storage Project Finance Underwriting in 2026' — COMMERCIAL BLOG, US market, https://sunraisecapital.com/blog/standalone-battery-storage-project-finance-underwriting-2026 ; GreenBridge Infrastructure, 'Debt Sizing', https://greenbridgeinfra.com/resources/project-finance/debt-sizing | LOW AUTHORITY and NOT GB. No authoritative GB source published DSCR levels for storage during this survey. Use the structure (dual P50/P90 test; lower leverage for merchant) rather than the specific numbers, and re-anchor the ratios to a GB term sheet before relying on them. The two sources disagree materially on the merchant DSCR (1.40x vs 1.75-2.00x). |
| Indicative senior debt pricing and equity return targets by structure | Contracted SOFR+200-275bps; hybrid SOFR+275-375bps; merchant SOFR+375-475bps. Equity IRR targets: tolling 9-11%, capacity-contracted 11-13%, hybrid hedge 13-15%, pure merchant 15-18%. Merchant equity premium 300-500bps over fully contracted. Mini-perm tenors of 7-10 years | bps over base rate; % equity IRR | United States, 2026. | SunRaise Capital, 'Standalone Battery Storage Project Finance Underwriting in 2026' — COMMERCIAL BLOG, US market | LOW AUTHORITY and NOT GB. Included only because the merchant-vs-contracted spread structure (300-500bps of equity premium for merchant) is corroborated in direction by the GB discount-rate practice of Gresham House (7.5% contracted vs 10.85-11.35% merchant, a spread of 335-385bps). |
| LFP battery warranty terms (2025 market) | 15-25 year warranties (previously 10-15); cycle warranties 10,000-12,000 cycles (previously 6,000-8,000) | years; cycles | Utility-scale LFP systems, global, 2025. | DNV, 'Financing the energy transition: building bankable battery projects', https://www.dnv.com/article/financing-energy-transition-building-bankable-battery-projects/ | Warranty length is not the same as bankable asset life; the warranty is only worth the manufacturer's covenant and only applies within its cycle and depth-of-discharge envelope. |
| GB BESS capacity degradation profile (modelling assumption) | 88% of original capacity at 2,500 cycles; 79% at 5,000; 72% at 7,500; approximately 67% at 10,000 cycles (~12-15 years) | % of original usable energy capacity | Generic lithium-ion assumption used in the GB revenue forecast; typical cycling constraint 1-2 cycles per day. Modelled BESS project life 15 years (vs 25 years for generation). | Modo Energy, 'Modelling Assumptions' (UK/Europe, July 2026 vintage), https://docs.modoenergy.com/pages/jul-2026/uk-europe/model-assumptions/ | A single generic curve; real degradation depends on chemistry, temperature, C-rate, depth of discharge and calendar age. Reconcile against the specific supplier warranty before use. |

England 2026/27 multipliers, non-RHL: 0.430 (RV under £51,000), 0.480 (RV £51,000-£499,999), 0.508 (RV £500,000+). Antecedent valuation date 1 April 2024; list effective 1 April 2026. Transitional relief caps 5% / 15% / 30% by RV band | % decapitalisation; £ in the pound multiplier | England (Wales differs). Battery storage carries Special Category Code 733 and is valued on the contractor's basis using Rating Cost Guide stage 1 costs, physical obsolescence allowances on the industrial scale, and land at prevailing industrial rates. | MHCLG, 'Business Rates Information Letter 3/2025: Confirmation of Decapitalisation Rates', 2025, https://www.gov.uk/government/publications/22025-confirmation-of-decapitalisation-rates ; Knight Frank, 'A Guide to 2026 Business Rates in England', 2026, https://www.knightfrank.co.uk/commercial/guides/business-rates-england ; Valuation Office Agency, 'Revaluation 2026 - everything you need to know', 29 September 2025 | The VOA has not published a £/MW or £/kW rateable value scheme for BESS. Exemptions require a generation licence and submission of a Non-Final Demand declaration; the DUoS capacity charge must be paid regardless. | Modo Energy, 'Electricity import charges: which do battery energy storage pay?', https://modoenergy.com/research/en/electricity-import-charges-battery-energy-storage | CRITICAL: the same source states that 'behind-the-meter batteries do not currently qualify for these exemptions'. For a BTM asset these amounts are costs, not avoided costs. The tariff values are 2023/24 vintage and must be refreshed. |
| GB BESS insurance cost | 0.5-3% of total system value per annum for commercial installations; a 1 MW commercial system indicatively £5,000-£30,000 per year; 10 MW+ projects at the lower end of the range; LFP 10-20% cheaper than other lithium chemistries; fire detection and suppression can reduce premiums 20-30% | % of insured value p.a.; £/yr | Commercial and utility-scale BESS, general market guidance. | Solarif, 'How much does battery storage fire insurance cost?', https://solarif.com/academy-article/how-much-does-battery-storage-fire-insurance-cost/ | LOW AUTHORITY (insurance broker marketing content). ## sweep2-batch-1

| Constant | Value | Units | Basis / conditions | Source | Caveat |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| DFS Minimum Submission Size / minimum Registered Unit Capacity | 0.1 | MW | Great Britain, effective 18 May 2026 (service expansion 9 April 2026). Registered Unit Capacity must be 'an amount (if not an integer, rounded to one decimal place) from and including zero point one (0.1) MW to and including one hundred (100) MW'. Applies to the DFS Unit, which may aggregate multiple Unit Meter Points provided all sit in the same DFS Zone. | NESO, 'Demand Flexibility Service Procurement Rules', v5.0, effective 18 May 2026, Schedule 1 and para 4.2, https://www.neso.energy/document/379391/download | This is the ONLY direct NESO revenue route below 1 MW. It is conditionally exclusive with other NESO services: rule 4.7.6 bars the meter point from forming part of a BM Unit other than a Supplier Base BMU, and 4.7.9 bars registration for other Balancing Services or DNO Flexibility Services where the DFS Stacking List prohibits it. |
| DFS Maximum Submission Size | 100 | MW | Great Britain, per DFS Unit, unless otherwise specified by NESO. | NESO, 'Demand Flexibility Service Procurement Rules', v5.0, 18 May 2026, Schedule 1 and para 4.2 | NESO reserves the right to specify a different maximum from time to time (para 11.5.7(b)). |
| DFS settlement value formula | S_ie = 0.5 * P_ie * Q_ie * K_ie | £ per DFS Unit per Contracted Settlement Period | Great Britain, from 9 April 2026. P_ie = Utilisation Price (£/MWh, pounds and pence each an integer) from the accepted DFS Bid; Q_ie = offered Service Volume (MW); the 0.5 converts a 30-minute settlement period to MWh. | NESO, 'Demand Flexibility Service Service Terms', v4.0, effective 9 April 2026, Schedule 1 (Utilisation Payments), https://www.neso.energy/document/379396/download | Payment is on OFFERED volume Q, scaled by performance K — so under-delivery is penalised through K, not through a reduced Q. |
| DFS performance factor K (piecewise) | K=1.2 if RV>=120; K=0.01*RV if 50<=RV<120; K=0.01*(2*RV-50) if 25<=RV<50; K=0 if 0<=RV<25; K=0.01*RV if -100<=RV<0; K=-1 if RV<-100, where RV = 100*V/(Q*0.5) | dimensionless multiplier on the settlement value | Great Britain, from 9 April 2026. V = DFS Settlement Volume (MWh) delivered against baseline; Q = offered Service Volume (MW). RV is realised delivery as a percentage of the contracted half-hour energy. | NESO, 'Demand Flexibility Service Service Terms', v4.0, 9 April 2026, Schedule 1 | K is capped above at 1.2 (20% over-delivery bonus) but is NEGATIVE below zero realised delivery, reaching -1 — i.e. the provider PAYS. Any model that clips K at zero understates downside. Note also the dead zone: delivering 0-25% of contracted volume earns exactly zero. |
| Dynamic Response (DC/DM/DR) minimum unit size | 1 | MW per response unit | Great Britain, current. Verbatim: 'A minimum response capacity of 1 MW is required per response unit.' Maximum 100 MW per response unit for DC, DM and DR. Applies to both BM and non-BM registered assets. | NESO, 'Response Services (DC/DM/DR) Provider Guidance', v14, June 2026, https://www.neso.energy/document/276606/download | Aggregation across assets is permitted only within the same GSP Group, and is subject to technical assurance. This is the gate that excludes sub-1 MW BTM assets from all six dynamic response products. |
| EAC / Dynamic Response order granularity | whole MW (integer) | MW | Great Britain, Enduring Auction Capability day-ahead co-optimised auctions for DCL, DCH, DML, DMH, DRL, DRH, plus Balancing Reserve (PBR/NBR), Quick Reserve (PQR/NQR) and Slow Reserve (PSR/NSR). Prices are £/MW/h and may be below £0/MW/h. | NESO, 'Response Services (DC/DM/DR) Provider Guidance', v14, June 2026 — 'Sell orders may only be submitted in whole MWs'; NESO, EAC auction rules pack, https://www.neso.energy/document/277671/download — child orders 'can be accepted for any integer volume between 0 and the total offered quantity' | Combined with the 1 MW floor this makes the ancillary-sellable quantity an integer in {0, 1, 2, ...}. Fractional nameplate above an integer boundary is unsellable into EAC. Physical Notifications into the BM are separately constrained: 'For BM, MW values must be declared as integers.' |
| Response Energy Volume (energy adequacy for energy-limited assets) | Response Energy Volume = Contracted Quantity x Delivery Duration; Delivery Duration = 15 min (DC), 30 min (DM), 60 min (DR) | MWh = MW x h | Great Britain, current. Applies to Energy Limited Units — assets whose State of Energy at the start of a Service Period is insufficient to provide full delivery of the Contracted Quantity for that period. Additional rules in section 6 of the Response Service Terms. | NESO, 'Response Services (DC/DM/DR) Provider Guidance', v14, June 2026 | This is the bilinear reserved_MW x duration term referenced in the sizing gap — it is a written rule, not a modelling convenience. It ties the MW that can be sold to the MWh available, which is exactly why duration is an optimisation output. |
| Energy Recovery obligation for energy-limited units | 20 | % of Response Energy Volume, per Settlement Period | Great Britain, current. Defined as 'the minimum volume of stored energy (or capability to store energy) capable of being recovered by way of State of Energy management in a single Settlement Period'. | NESO, 'Response Services (DC/DM/DR) Provider Guidance', v14, June 2026 | Consumes headroom that would otherwise be available for arbitrage or peak shaving in the same settlement period. Must be reserved, not merely tracked. |
| Frequency response availability factor threshold | F = 0 if availability < 0.999 within the Settlement Period; equivalently ~1.8 seconds of unavailability zeroes the whole 30-minute period | dimensionless; seconds | Great Britain, current. 0.1% of 1800 s = 1.8 s. Performance monitoring uses 20 Hz sampled data for DC, DM and Stacking Services (DR at 20 Hz or 2 Hz), with a rolling-minimum error window per service. | NESO, 'Response Services (DC/DM/DR) Provider Guidance', v14, June 2026 — 'F_ije is zero where there is any period or periods of unavailability within Settlement Period j'; 'if a unit has an availability of less than 99.9% then availability factor will be 0 for that settlement period'; 'For 20Hz sampled data, a full SP contains 36000 rows of data' | The EFA-block performance factor is K_e = min_j k_j — the MINIMUM across the block — so one degraded settlement period sets the payment for the whole four-hour block. This is why annual availability scalars are invalid for this revenue line. |
| Response service aggregation geographic restriction | same GSP Group only | n/a | Great Britain, current, for DC/DM/DR. | NESO, 'Response Services (DC/DM/DR) Provider Guidance', v14, June 2026 — 'Aggregation is possible when assets sit behind same GSP Group (subject to technical assurance being carried out on the risks associated with moving to GSP Group)' | Makes response aggregation location-tied: relocating an asset across a GSP Group boundary breaks the aggregation. Directly relevant to the host-default / relocation loss-given-default case. |
| Slow Reserve minimum delivery duration | 2 hours (4 consecutive service windows) | hours; 30-minute service windows | Great Britain, Slow Reserve (PSR/NSR) via EAC; go-live and first auction reported for 31 March 2026. Service windows are 30 minutes; EFA blocks are 4 hours (8 windows). | NESO, EAC auction rules pack, https://www.neso.energy/document/277671/download — Flexible Group feature: 'Minimum Consecutive Windows ... must be at least 4 service windows (i.e., 2 hours) for PSR/NSR, with a default value of 4 service windows' | Exclusive to Slow Reserve — not applicable to DC/DM/DR, Balancing Reserve or Quick Reserve. Imposes a 2-hour minimum energy adequacy at the contracted MW, which is a binding duration constraint for short-duration BTM assets. |
| G99 reactive power capability requirement | 0.95 lagging to 0.95 leading power factor, continuous, at Registered Capacity | power factor (dimensionless) | Great Britain distribution connections, Power Generating Modules between 1 MW and 10 MW, measured at the connection point, when operating at Registered Capacity. | Energy Networks Association, 'Engineering Recommendation G99', Issue 2 (2025), https://dcode.org.uk/assets/250307ena-erec-g99-issue-2-(2025).pdf; summarised in Aurora Power Consulting, 'G99 Reactive Power Requirements', https://aurora-power.co.uk/g99-reactive-power/ | cos(phi)=0.95 implies Q/S = 0.3122, so the mandated reactive capability alone consumes 5.0% of real-power headroom when fully used. The vendor PQ capability chart is usually temperature- and voltage-dependent and may be tighter than G99 at ambient extremes. |
| Real-power derate from reactive dispatch (inverter apparent-power circle) | Q=0.3122*S -> P_max=0.950*S (5.0% derate); Q=0.5*S -> P_max=0.866*S (13.4%); Q=0.6*S -> P_max=0.800*S (20.0%) | per-unit of S_rated | Any grid-following inverter operating within its apparent-power rating. Derived, not measured. | Derived directly from P^2+Q^2<=S^2; capability requirement from ENA EREC G99 Issue 2 (2025) | The derate applies simultaneously to every MW-denominated revenue line in that half hour (ancillary contracted quantity, BM MEL/MIL, CM deliverability, DFS Service Volume). A regular K-gon inner approximation of the circle is conservative by 1-cos(pi/K): 3.4% at K=8, 1.5% at K=12, 0.9% at K=16. |
| GB final consumption levies and network charges avoidable by a licensed, Non-Final-Demand storage site | CfD ~£13/MWh; RO ~£27/MWh; FiT ~£8/MWh; CCL £7.80/MWh; CM supplier charge + settlement costs levy ~£11/MWh; BSUoS £13.41/MWh (summer) and £14.03/MWh (winter); AAHEDC £0.42/MWh (NOT avoidable) | £/MWh of imported (charging) energy | Great Britain, charge year 2023/24 for CCL and BSUoS as stated; other figures as reported. Exemptions require a Generation Licence (for final consumption levies and CCL) and a Non-Final Demand declaration to NESO (for BSUoS and TNUoS/DUoS fixed residuals). | Modo Energy, 'Electricity import charges: battery energy storage', https://www.modoenergy.com/research/en/electricity-import-charges-battery-energy-storage | CRITICAL: the same source states these exemptions do 'not apply to storage located Behind-the-Meter. These batteries are currently unlikely to qualify for these exemptions.' The stack totals roughly £80/MWh (~8 p/kWh) on charging, so the separate-storage-MPAN decision is worth that amount per charged MWh if the exemption can be obtained. Vintage is 2023/24 — re-obtain current-year values before use. Reputable industry analytics, not a primary code document; cross-check against Elexon FCL guidance and the DCUSA/CUSC as-published rates. |
| FiT deemed export percentage and eligibility threshold | 50% (solar PV), 75% (hydro); available only where Total Installed Capacity is 30 kW or less | % of generation meter reading; kW | Great Britain, FiT scheme. Deemed export applies to 'Accredited FIT installations with a TIC of 30kW or less, where it is not possible or practical for export to be measured via an export meter'. | Ofgem, 'Guidance for generators: Co-location of electricity storage and hydrogen production under the RO, FIT, REGO and SEG', v6.2, May 2025, paras 3.4 and 3.17-3.18, https://www.ofgem.gov.uk/sites/default/files/2025-06/co_location_guidance_for_generators_v6.2_20250528135013.pdf | Materially limits the panel's concern for C&I: a typical C&I rooftop array exceeds 30 kW and is therefore on METERED export, where the binding risk is total loss of export payments if the export meter cannot be shown to measure only FiT-installation output, not distortion of a deemed percentage. |
| FiT/RO co-located storage metering prohibition | Net values may NOT be derived from separate input and output meters; a single approved bi-directional meter performing the net calculation is required where grid charging is possible | n/a (rule) | Great Britain, FiT (and analogous RO) accredited installations with co-located storage. Verbatim: 'The generation meter reading cannot be calculated using separate input and output meters to calculate a net output value'; 'Using multiple meters to calculate a net output value is not permitted.' | Ofgem, co-location guidance v6.2, May 2025, paras 3.8, 3.14, 3.19, 3.25, 3.26, 3.30 | Ofgem notes 'most smart meters are not bi-directional meters under the definition we use here'. Consequences of failure: no generation payments (para 3.16) and/or no export payments (3.29). Additionally, bi-directional netting is monthly, so 'If the difference between import and export in one month is negative, zero FITs payments could be claimed' (3.27), and round-trip losses mean 'FIT payments are likely to be reduced' (3.28). |
| PV module degradation rate | 0.5%/year median (all technologies pooled); ~0.5%/yr crystalline silicon, ~1.0%/yr other technologies | % of rated output per year | Global field data: nearly 2000 measured degradation rates on individual modules and whole systems in the 2012 review; over 11,000 rates across ~200 studies in 40 countries in the compendium. Not GB-specific. | D. C. Jordan and S. R. Kurtz, 'Photovoltaic Degradation Rates — An Analytical Review', NREL/JA-5200-51664, June 2012 / Prog. Photovolt: Res. Appl., https://www.nrel.gov/docs/fy12osti/51664.pdf; extended in NREL 'Compendium of Photovoltaic Degradation Rates' | Compounded over a 15-year underwriting horizon: 0.995^15 = 92.8% at 0.5%/yr versus 0.860^... i.e. 0.99^15 = 86.0% at 1.0%/yr — a 6.8 percentage-point spread on year-15 output. Use the module warranty's guaranteed degradation curve as the downside bound and the measured median as the base case, and state which. |
| NFCC BESS siting parameters | 30 m initial minimum separation from occupied buildings; 3 ft (0.914 m) between BESS units absent UL 9540A no-propagation evidence; no vertical stacking; 25 L/s hydrant flow or ~180,000 L static water (120 minutes) | metres; litres/second; litres | England, Wales and Northern Ireland FRS guidance. The 30 m figure is an initial proposed distance before mitigation such as blast walls, derived from NFPA 855's 100 ft remote-installation distance. Automatic detection (smoke, gas, radiant heat) with continuous combustible-gas monitoring is expected. | National Fire Chiefs Council, 'Grid Scale Battery Energy Storage System planning — Guidance for Fire and Rescue Services', approved for publication December 2025 (supersedes 2023 v1), https://nfcc.org.uk/our-services/building-safety/grid-scale-energy-storage-system-planning-guidance-for-fire-and-rescue-services/ | Titled for GRID-SCALE BESS; its direct application to a 0.1-5 MW BTM unit adjacent to an occupied C&I building is not settled, but the host's property insurer will very likely reference it or NFPA 855. FRSs are NOT statutory planning consultees, so no objection is not evidence of acceptability — the binding constraint is the insurer. On a constrained C&I site a 30 m standoff is frequently project-determinative. |
| Electricity storage NSIP threshold removal | England 50 MW and Wales 350 MW thresholds disapplied for electricity storage other than pumped hydro | MW | England and Wales. Amends the Planning Act 2008 so that storage is not a Nationally Significant Infrastructure Project at any capacity (pumped hydro excepted). | The Infrastructure Planning (Electricity Storage Facilities) Order 2020, SI 2020/1218, made 4 November 2020, in force 2 December 2020, https://www.legislation.gov.uk/uksi/2020/1218/made | Means a BTM battery is always a local planning matter (or permitted development, which requires its own analysis) — never an NSIP. Scotland has a separate consenting regime under the Electricity Act 1989. |
| SSES Load Control Licence scope and NIS threshold | In scope: private EVs/EVSCPs, smart-mandate heat technologies, and 'domestic-scale BESS'. Load controllers managing <300 MW of load: Load Control Licence cyber conditions. Load controllers managing >=300 MW: additionally NIS Regulations as Operators of Essential Services. | MW of controlled load | Great Britain (England, Scotland, Wales). Applications open March 2027; licensing requirement effective March 2028 (subject to Parliamentary scrutiny). All in-scope controllers must meet a Cyber Assessment Framework profile proportionate to risk. Elexon is the minded-to enduring governance body via BSC modifications. | DESNZ, 'Delivering a smart and secure electricity system: consultation response', https://assets.publishing.service.gov.uk/media/6808a2630324470d6a394eb2/SSES-consultation-response.pdf | A 0.1-5 MW C&I battery is NOT a domestic-scale BESS and is out of scope — this closes the gap for the ASSET. The exposure sits on the optimiser if it also controls domestic-scale ESAs, and on any optimiser crossing the 300 MW controlled-load threshold. Government response on draft regulations published 7 August 2026 proposing three class exemptions. |
| GB Capacity Market payment suspension precedent | Standstill from 15 November 2018 to 24 October 2019 (~11 months) with no payments made to agreement holders | months; dates | Great Britain. The UK confirmed in December 2018 that it would 'operate the capacity market as normal, but without payments being made to agreement holders'. The Commission opened an in-depth investigation on 21 February 2019 and re-approved on 24 October 2019. The Court of Justice subsequently set aside the General Court judgment on 2 September 2021 (C-57/19 P). | Judgment of the General Court, Case T-793/14, Tempus Energy v Commission, 15 November 2018, ECLI:EU:T:2018:790, https://eur-lex.europa.eu/legal-content/EN/TXT/?uri=CELEX:62014TJ0793; European Commission State aid SA.35980, positive decision 24 October 2019, Commission Decision (EU) 2020/348, https://eur-lex.europa.eu/eli/dec/2020/348/oj/eng; Florence School of Regulation, 'The GB capacity market standstill', https://fsr.eui.eu/the-gb-capacity-market-standstill-what-is-happening-and-why-2/ | Deferred payments were reported at the order of £1 billion but I could not confirm the figure from a primary source — treat the magnitude as UNSOURCED and the dates/mechanism as sourced. Post-Brexit the scheme sits under the Subsidy Control Act 2022 with a challenge route to the Competition Appeal Tribunal, so the legal mechanism of a repeat differs, but the exposure (a total, temporary suspension of a supposedly firm revenue line) persists. |
| UK company insolvency base rate | 52.5 per 10,000 active companies (0.525% per year); 'equivalent to one in 190 companies entering insolvency' | insolvencies per 10,000 companies per year | United Kingdom, 12 months to December 2025, all registered companies. October 2025 figure 53.4, August 2025 figure 52.6 — the rate has been stable in the low 50s. | The Insolvency Service, 'Company Insolvency Statistics', December 2025, published 27 January 2026, https://www.gov.uk/government/collections/insolvency-service-official-statistics | This is a POPULATION base rate and is a floor, not an estimate, for any specific host. Use the host's own credit score and the sector default rate for its SIC code. At 0.525%/yr the cumulative probability of at least one host default over a 15-year term is ~7.7% for an average-risk company; a single-site C&I host in a cyclical sector will be materially higher. |
| C&I electricity supply contract volume tolerance band | 80% to 120% of forecast consumption (plus or minus 20%) | % of contracted/forecast annual volume | Great Britain, non-domestic fixed-price electricity supply contracts. Breaching the band triggers a reconciliation or mark-to-market charge on the out-of-band volume. | Cost Advice, 'Volume tolerance clauses', https://www.costadvice.co.uk/latest-news/volume-tolerance-clauses/ — 'Most suppliers have an arbitrary 80/120 threshold set'; corroborated by Meet George, 'Volume tolerance: the cheapest contract trap for SME', https://meetgeorge.co.uk/blog/volume-tolerance-cheapest-contract-trap-sme — 'typically within an 80-120% band' | Broker and consultancy commentary, NOT primary contract documentation — treat as an indicative default only. Bands vary widely by supplier and contract size and some large C&I contracts are tighter. The actual band and the reconciliation formula MUST be read from the host's executed contract. A battery changes both volume and shape, so it can breach in both directions within the same contract year. |
| Capacity Market storage de-rating factor by duration (indicative shape) | 0.5h ~4-5%; 2h ~19-22% (T-1) and ~30-40% (T-4); 8h+ ~90-95% | % of connection/nameplate capacity | Great Britain, T-1 and T-4 Capacity Market auctions, factors as published in the ESO Electricity Capacity Report May 2023 vintage. Duration is binned; a provider may bid at a lower duration bin than its physical capability. | Energy-Storage.news, 'UK system operator reassessing outdated Capacity Market de-rating factors for storage', reporting NESO/ESO Electricity Capacity Report (May 2023), https://www.energy-storage.news/uk-system-operator-reassessing-outdated-capacity-market-de-rating-factors-for-storage/; corroborated by Solar Power Portal, https://www.solarpowerportal.co.uk/energy-storage/national-grid-revisits-de-rating-factors-for-energy-storage | SECONDARY SOURCE and 2023 vintage — the bin-by-bin table for the specific target auction MUST be taken from the NESO Electricity Capacity Report and the DESNZ auction parameters letter for that auction year. T-1 and T-4 factors differ materially for the same duration. The key structural point for sizing is confirmed regardless: CM de-rated capacity is driven by DURATION (energy) while ancillary and BM revenue is driven by POWER, so optimal duration is a price ratio. |
| Capacity Market Extended Performance Test frequency for storage | at least once every three years, during at least one Satisfactory Performance Day | years | Great Britain, Storage Generating Technology Class CMUs. New build units awarded agreements after 21 December 2017 are subject to the same requirement. SPDs occur during winter. | EMR Delivery Body, 'Agreement Management — Existing' and 'Agreement Management — New Build', https://emrdeliverybody.nationalenergyso.com/IG/s/article/Agreement-Management-Existing — 'A Capacity Committed CMU with a Storage Generating Technology Class ... must demonstrate Extended Performance during at least one Satisfactory Performance Day'; The Electricity Capacity (Amendment) Regulations 2025, SI 2025/183 explanatory memorandum — 'EPTs are a subfunction of the SPD requirement' | Elexon CMAG CP370 proposes revising the EPT methodology to better reflect storage contracted volume and duration — check its status before relying on the current form. The underwriting-relevant point is that this is a DATED test: an outage falling on the SPD/EPT window risks termination, which is categorically different from losing hours of revenue, and cannot be represented by an annual availability scalar. |
| BESS decommissioning / de-installation unit cost (indicative) | ~$50/kW decommissioning and demolition; ~$38/kW material disposition | USD per kW | United States, grid-scale (120 MW), trade-press estimate. Derived by dividing the quoted totals by 120 MW. | POWER Magazine, 'BESS Decommissioning and End-of-Life Planning: Why Care Today?', https://www.powermag.com/blog/bess-decommissioning-and-end-of-life-planning-why-care-today/ — $1.4m decommissioning and demolition plus $4.6m material disposition for a 120 MW BESS site | US costs at grid scale — NOT transferable to a 0.1-5 MW GB BTM installation without a substantial small-scale multiplier, and it omits transport and reinstallation entirely. Use only as a lower bound on the de-installation component of the host-default loss-given-default case, and state the multiplier applied. The dominant LGD component for BTM is usually not the physical cost but the permanent loss of location-tied contracts (CM agreement, DNO flexibility zone, DFS Zone, GSP Group aggregation). |
| DNV-verified BESS fleet availability (vendor) | 98.7% MW-weighted (earlier verification); 99.3% reported for large projects (2026) | % availability | Global, utility-scale Fluence fleet, MW-weighted, third-party verified by DNV. | Energy-Storage.news, 'DNV verifies 98.7% availability of Fluence's global battery energy storage fleet', https://www.energy-storage.news/dnv-verifies-98-7-availability-of-fluences-global-battery-energy-storage-fleet/; Fluence Investor Relations, 'DNV validates Fluence 99% fleet performance availability', https://ir.fluenceenergy.com/news-releases/news-release-details/dnv-validates-fluence-99-fleet-performance-availability-large; DNV, '2024 Battery Scorecard', https://www.dnv.com/publications/2024-battery-scorecard/ | STRONG selection bias — vendor-supplied, single-vendor, utility-scale, and MW-weighted (so dominated by large well-maintained sites). It is an annual scalar and therefore does not answer the question that matters: WHEN the outages fell. IEEE work states 'forced outage characteristics of ESS remain largely unexplored'. Do not use as the availability input for a BTM asset; use it only to sanity-check the order of magnitude of the equipment-cause component, and add the BTM-specific causes (host planned shutdown, comms link, aggregator platform) separately. |

## DFS realised outturn — NESO's own winter 2024/25 review (added 2026-08-17, Olly)

The row that unblocks DFS. `ancillary_services.dfs_performance_factor_k_piecewise` carried only a
£4,559/MWh 2022-23 anchor, and the codebase refused to annualise DFS because no called-event count
was sourced. NESO's winter 2024/25 review publishes **both** — the event count and their own
per-1-MW annual revenue scenarios — and in doing so shows the £4,559 anchor is **25.6× too high** as
an annualisation basis for the current service. Deriving from these rows is sourcing; deriving from
the anchor would have produced ~£538/kW/yr, roughly 5× the entire grid-scale GB BESS index.

| Constant | Value | Units | Basis / conditions | Source | Caveat |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| DFS called-event count per season (the term that annualises DFS) | 56 Service Requirements published covering 306 settlement periods; volume actually procured on **44 events covering 236 settlement periods** | events; settlement periods per winter season | Great Britain, 27 November 2024 – 28 March 2025 (Ofgem approval 21 November 2024), national DFS under the merit-based margin-tool design. Verbatim: 'Throughout the winter (27.11.24 – 28.03.25), 56 Service Requirements were published, covering 306 settlement periods (SP), with volume procured on 44 events covering 236 settlement periods.' | NESO, 'Demand Flexibility Service (DFS)' [winter 2024/25 review], Public, published 3 July 2025, https://www.neso.energy/document/363911/download, pp. 3, 5 and 16 | This is a NATIONAL count of settlement periods in which NESO procured ANY volume — it is an UPPER BOUND on the periods a given unit is called, because acceptance is per-bid and (from 9 April 2026) per-zone against a Zonal Cap that 'may be zero (0) MW'. Vintage: this season PREDATES the 9 April 2026 zonal, bi-directional redesign and the 0.1 MW eligibility floor. |
| DFS average accepted utilisation price (realised) | **£210/MWh** average accepted bid across all accepted bids; range £59/MWh (27 Dec 2024, 1 MW, 1 SP) to £1,290/MWh (8 Jan 2025, 18:00–19:00); five highest daily clears £1,290, £750, £740, £575, £572 | GBP/MWh | Great Britain, winter 2024/25 as above. NESO's own stated average. Independent arithmetic check: £1,233,312 accepted ÷ 5,449.6 MWh accepted = £226/MWh, consistent with the stated £210 (the two differ because the stated figure is a simple mean over bids and the quotient is volume-weighted). | NESO, 'Demand Flexibility Service (DFS)' winter 2024/25 review, 3 July 2025, https://www.neso.energy/document/363911/download, p. 16 | **SUPERSEDES the £4,559/MWh 2022-23 anchor as a forward basis** (register line 230, Cornwall Insight, Dec 2023). The service repriced by roughly 20× when it moved from a winter-contingency enhanced action to a merit-based margin tool, so the two figures are not comparable and the older one must never be annualised. Even the single highest accepted bid of the whole season (£1,290) is 3.5× below the old anchor. |
| DFS aggregate realised performance factor K (fleet) | **0.765** = £943,983 settled ÷ £1,233,312 accepted; £76,665 withheld by performance monitoring | dimensionless | Great Britain, winter 2024/25, whole-fleet aggregate across 28 providers and 1.98m registered MPANs. Derived by division of two figures stated verbatim in the Financial Summary. | NESO, 'Demand Flexibility Service (DFS)' winter 2024/25 review, 3 July 2025, https://www.neso.energy/document/363911/download, pp. 3, 5 and 16 | **A FLEET STATISTIC, NOT AN ASSET'S EXPECTED K.** The population is 90% domestic MPANs and 99.58% manually instructed; an automated BTM battery should sit materially above it. Do not apply it to NESO's per-MW scenarios below — those already embed accepted-bid pricing, so multiplying would double-count the haircut. Also note the source's own internal discrepancy: p.3 states £1,223,312 accepted where p.5 and p.16 state £1,233,312; the Financial Summary (p.16) figure is used here and both are recorded. |
| DFS revenue per 1 MW per season (NESO's published scenarios) | S1 full participation, all 306 SPs: low £17,918 / avg **£21,009** / high £25,609 (highest accepted bid) or £33,560 (breakpoints). S2 one hour per Service Requirement, 112 SPs: low £3,142 / avg **£7,558** / high £10,166 (or £12,477). S3 the 40 highest-priced SPs: £12,337 (or £13,726). | GBP per MW per winter season (≡ GBP/kW/yr ÷ 1000) | Great Britain, winter 2024/25. Verbatim: 'The following scenarios assume a single DFS unit with 1 MW of flexibility that meets the DFS service requirements.' Linear in offered volume Q, because settlement is S = 0.5·P·Q·K. | NESO, 'Demand Flexibility Service (DFS)' winter 2024/25 review, 3 July 2025, https://www.neso.energy/document/363911/download, pp. 17–18 | **NESO's own caveat, verbatim: 'these have been created to act as a guide and are not indicative of future year revenue as these are based off the system and market conditions from the winter 24/25 period.'** So: £3.1–33.6/kW/yr, with £7.6/kW/yr (S2 avg) the defensible central case for a unit that responds to most Service Requirements but does not win every settlement period, and £21.0/kW/yr (S1 avg) the ceiling at full acceptance. Values exclude stacked revenue. The scenarios also predate the zonal redesign. |
| DFS stacking as actually operated (NOT a blanket exclusion) | Stacking facilitated with 'all the core DNO Flexibility Services in addition to the Capacity Market (CM), Local Constraints Market (LCM) and several other trial areas such as Crowdflex'; 10 providers stacking MPANs with both CM (11,936) and LCM (3,114) | count of providers; count of MPANs | Great Britain, winter 2024/25, following the EBR Article 18 consultation. | NESO, 'Demand Flexibility Service (DFS)' winter 2024/25 review, 3 July 2025, https://www.neso.energy/document/363911/download, p. 6 | **Materially qualifies the exclusivity reading.** Procurement Rules 4.7.9 bars registration for other Balancing Services or DNO Flexibility Services **only where the DFS Stacking List prohibits it** — a conditional bar, and NESO's own review says core DNO flexibility stacking WAS facilitated. The one unconditional bar is 4.7.6 (the meter point may not form part of a BM Unit other than a Supplier Base BMU); 4.7.8(a) expressly permits the CM. **DATA ASK: the current DFS Stacking List**, which is the governing document and is not held in this repo. GridQ nonetheless models DFS and DNO flex non-additively pending that document — a deliberately conservative modelling choice, not a statement that the rules forbid stacking. |
